It seems many segments are close in a repeating pattern maybe due to beta sheet. But we need more precise.

Maybe the protein is a beta-propeller? No, 57 aa too small. Could be a beta-hairpin stack. Let's inspect Cα trace visually from coordinates. It seems to wind around, maybe forming a beta sandwich with strands:
1-5, 9-15, 19-23, 27-31, 35-39, 43-47, 51-55? That's many short strands alternating with turns. Could be a beta-solenoid or beta-helix? Wait 57 aa with many repeats of extended segments separated by turns every 4 residues. The Cα trace seems to alternate direction, maybe forming a beta-helix or beta-spiral. Let's examine sequence and Cα path: residues 1-5 go one direction, 5-9 another, 9-15 another, 15-19 another, 19-23 another, 23-27 another, 27-31 another, 31-35 another, 35-39 another, 39-43 another, 43-47 another, 47-51 another, 51-55 another, 55-57. This looks like a series of beta hairpins or a beta-solenoid with repeating turns. Could be a "beta-roll" or "beta-helix" structure, which is predominantly beta sheet. Many bacterial toxins or antifreeze proteins have beta-helices with repeating turns. But 57 aa with 10 cysteines? Maybe a "beta-defensin" with beta-hairpins.

Let's inspect if there are regular beta-strand hydrogen bonds between residues separated by 2 in sequence? Actually in a beta-hairpin, interstrand H-bonds occur between residues i and j where j is across the strand, often i+2 or i+3. We can compute O_i to N_j distances for j = i+2 or i+3 maybe. So there are many O_i to N_{i+2} distances around 3.1-3.5, indicating beta-turns or short hairpins. But beta sheet H-bonds are usually between strands, not i to i+2. However in a beta-hairpin, residues across strands can be separated by 2 in sequence if the turn is tight. For example, in a beta-hairpin with two residues in turn, strand residue i can H-bond to residue i+2 across the turn? Actually if the chain reverses, the residue across from i might be i+3 or i+4, not i+2. But O_i to N_{i+2} could be a beta-turn hydrogen bond (i to i+2) stabilizing a tight turn. Many such distances suggest turns, not sheet.

Maybe the structure is a series of beta-turns, not a beta sheet. But the question asks classify chain as predominantly alpha helical, beta sheet, or mixed alpha/beta. If there are many beta-turns but no long helices or sheets, maybe still beta sheet? Need infer overall fold.

Let's inspect if there are interstrand H-bonds between residues separated by more than 2. We can search O_i to N_j for j-i > 2. Let's compute some likely pairs from Cα proximity. For each residue i, find j with Cα distance ~4.5-6.5 and sequence separation >2. Then check O/N distances.

This close Cα pair list is not very helpful because many residues are close due to compactness, not necessarily sheet.

Maybe we can compute actual secondary structure using known algorithms. Since no helices, likely beta. But let's not jump. Could be a small protein with irregular fold, maybe "mixed alpha/beta" if there is a short helix we missed because coordinates maybe not ideal? Let's inspect Cα distances for helical patterns more carefully. Alpha helix has i,i+4 Cα distance ~6.2, but our computed i,i+4 distances are mostly >7 or <5. However some segments have i,i+4 around 5-7: 26->30 5.3, 27->31 6.7, 41->45 5.8, 42->46 7.0, 45->49 13.0, 51->55 6.9. Could there be a short alpha helix around 26-31? Let's inspect backbone dihedrals or H-bonds. We computed O_i to N_{i+4} distances for 26->30 5.8, 27->31 5.4, not H-bond. But maybe because O and N labels are from author numbering and chain is not standard? No, should be fine. Alpha helix H-bond O_i to N_{i+4} should be ~2.8. None. So no alpha helix.

Could there be pi helix? i,i+5 H-bonds? Unlikely. But classification categories don't include pi. Yet if there is a pi helix and beta sheet, maybe mixed? But probably not.

Let's inspect if there are beta sheet H-bonds. We can search O_i to N_j for j-i > 2 and distance <3.5. Let's do systematically for likely close Cα pairs. We can compute O_i to N_j and O_j to N_i for pairs with Cα distance ~4.5-6.5. Let's pick pairs from close list with separation >2 and Cα distance <6.5. There are many. We'll check a subset that might form sheet.

Many close pairs. But to identify beta sheet, we need H-bonds between backbone atoms of residues on different strands. Let's compute O_i to N_j for some pairs with Cα distance ~4-6 and sequence separation >2. We can look for distances <3.5. So there are many H-bonds, but many are local i+2 or i+3. The long-range ones 1-17, 3-15, 5-13 suggest a beta sheet between N-terminal strand 1-5 and residues 13-17. Let's see if there are more H-bonds between these strands: maybe 2-16? We saw no. 4-14? no. 6-12? no. So maybe only three H-bonds, enough for a small antiparallel beta sheet of two strands. Also 27-30 and 44-46 etc are local turns. Could there be another beta sheet involving residues 35-55? Let's inspect long-range H-bonds beyond i+3. We found 1-17, 3-15, 5-13. Also maybe 27-30 is i+3, 44-46 i+2, 52-54 i+2, 53-55 i+2, 55-57 i+2. Not long-range. But there may be other long-range H-bonds we haven't computed because Cα distances >6.5 but still H-bond possible? Usually H-bond distance implies Cα distance around 4.5-6.5, but could be up to 7. Let's search more systematically for O_i to N_j distances <3.5 for j-i >=4. We can compute from close Cα pairs with distance <7.5 maybe. Let's list pairs with Cα distance <7.5 and separation >=4. We already have many. Let's compute O_i-N_j for those not yet done.
